Trivial extension DG-algebras, unitally positive A ∞ A_{\infty} -algebras, and applications
Joseph Karmazyn, Emma Lepri, Michael Wemyss

Abstract
. To any periodic module over any algebra, this paper introduces an associated trivial extension DG -algebra 𝒯 \mathcal{T} . After first passing to a strictly unital A ∞ A_{\infty} minimal model, it then constructs a particular A ∞ A_{\infty} -algebra 𝒩 \mathcal{N} , called the unitally positive A ∞ A_{\infty} -algebra, which roughly speaking describes the identity in degree zero and all the positive cohomology. The object 𝒩 \mathcal{N} is fundamental, and can be constructed for any DG -category satisfying very mild assumptions. The main application is to birational geometry. When applied to contraction algebras, the construction gives a simple and direct proof of the Donovan–Wemyss conjecture, namely that smooth irreducible 3 3 -fold flops are classified by their contraction algebras, and thus by noncommutative data.



1.
Introduction

Surgeries that preserve the birational class of a variety are fundamental to algebraic geometry. In dimension two, contractions of (
-1
)-curves in smooth surfaces were known to the Italian School in the 1890s. Dimension three is much harder: it took a further eighty years, at the advent of the modern minimal model programme in the late 1970s, for the next fundamentally new surgeries to be discovered, namely smooth
3
-fold flops.

Over 45 years on from that discovery, this paper is devoted to giving a simple and direct proof of the conjecture
[
DW1
]
, which predicted that smooth
3
-fold flops can in fact be classified, using noncommutative data. The novelty in this paper is to prove the conjecture by constructing and controlling two new (derived) objects: first the trivial extension
DG
-algebra
\mathcal{T}
, then a particular ‘unitally positive’
A_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathcal{N}
.

Happily,
\mathcal{T}
can be constructed in very general periodic settings, and the main character
\mathcal{N}
can be defined for very general
DG
-categories. As such, neither construction is specific to birational geometry, and we so begin by outlining both in detail.

1.1.
The trivial extension
DG
-algebra

Consider any algebra
\Upgamma
, and any
M\in\mathop{\textnormal{mod}}\nolimits\Upgamma
such that there exists a complex of finitely generated projective
\Upgamma
-modules

\mathrm{P}=(P_{n-1}\to P_{n-2}\to\ldots\to P_{1}\to P_{0})
where

0\to M\xrightarrow{\upbeta}P_{n-1}\to P_{n-2}\to\dots\to P_{1}\to P_{0}\xrightarrow{\upalpha}M\to 0

is exact. In other words, consider any periodic
\Upgamma
-module
M
. The complex
\mathrm{P}
is said to have length
n
. Mildly abusing notation, by shifting the complex
\mathrm{P}
and using the connecting map
d_{0}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\upbeta\circ\upalpha
, consider next the complex

\mathcal{P}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\quad\dots\to\mathrm{P}[2n]\xrightarrow{d_{0}[n]}\mathrm{P}[n]\xrightarrow{d_{0}}\mathrm{P}\to 0

where each component
\mathrm{P}[n\ell]
represents a length
n
complex of projective
\Upgamma
-modules. The complex
\mathcal{P}
gives a (possibly non-minimal) projective resolution of
M
. From this, consider the usual
DG
-algebra

\mathcal{E}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathcal{P})
,
and the element
\upsigma\in\mathcal{E}
defined as

\upsigma\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\hbox to310.06pt{\vbox to52.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 155.02998pt\lower-26.31947pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Write
{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}^{i}
, where

{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}^{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{x\in\mathcal{E}^{i}\mid\upsigma\circ x=(-1)^{|\upsigma||x|}x\circ\upsigma\}
and
\mathcal{E}^{i}
is the
i
th graded piece of
\mathcal{E}
, recalled in
2.1
. There is a similar version
{{}^{{\scriptstyle-}\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
, which accounts for an additional sign; see
2.8
for details.

The trivial extension
DG
-algebra
\mathcal{T}
is then defined as follows. As a graded vector space,
\mathcal{T}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}\oplus{{}^{{\scriptstyle-}\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}[1-n]
, whilst multiplication is defined on homogeneous elements by

(x,y)\cdot(a,b)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\big(x\circ a,\,x\circ b+(-1)^{|a|(|\upsigma|+1)}y\circ a\big).

Up to ignoring signs and shifts, the above is precisely the multiplication on the classical trivial extension algebra. The main point here, and perhaps the main novelty, is that with the signs and shifts, there exists a differential
\upxi
on
\mathcal{T}
defined by

\upxi(x,y)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\big(\updelta(x)-(-1)^{|x|}y\circ\upsigma,\,\updelta(y)\big).

We explain in
2.12
that
\upxi
can be viewed as the differential on a particular cone.

Our first result asserts that the multiplication and differential are compatible.

Proposition 1.1

(
2.13
)
.

(\mathcal{T},\cdot,\upxi)
is a
DG
-algebra.

Constructing
DG
-algebras which are simultaneously explicit and useful is surprisingly difficult. One way of interpreting
1.1
is that it gives a new way of constructing a
DG
-algebra, whenever one is faced with some form of periodic behaviour.

One of the features of the
DG
-algebra
\mathcal{T}
is that it has cohomology in both positive and negative degrees.

Proposition 1.2

(
3.8
)
.

If the natural map
\textnormal{Hom}_{\Upgamma}(M,M)\to\textnormal{Ext}^{n}_{\Upgamma}(M,M)
in
3.7
is injective, then the only non-zero cohomology groups of
\mathcal{T}
are the following, where in each degree take the direct sum of the groups stated.

\textnormal{H}^{-(n-1)}(\mathcal{T})

\ldots

\textnormal{H}^{-1}(\mathcal{T})

\textnormal{H}^{0}(\mathcal{T})

\textnormal{H}^{1}(\mathcal{T})

\ldots

\textnormal{H}^{n-2}(\mathcal{T})

\textnormal{H}^{n-1}(\mathcal{T})

\textnormal{Hom}_{\Upgamma}(M,M)

\ldots

\textnormal{Ext}^{n-2}_{\Upgamma}(M,M)

\textnormal{Ext}^{n-1}_{\Upgamma}(M,M)

\textnormal{Hom}_{\Upgamma}(M,M)

\textnormal{Ext}^{1}_{\Upgamma}(M,M)

\ldots

\textnormal{Ext}^{n-2}_{\Upgamma}(M,M)

\textnormal{Ext}^{n-1}_{\Upgamma}(M,M)

In (almost) all of our applications the map
\textnormal{Hom}_{\Upgamma}(M,M)\to\textnormal{Ext}^{n}_{\Upgamma}(M,M)
is indeed injective, however we do also compute the cohomology of
\mathcal{T}
in general. When the map is not injective the answer is just mildly more technical to state; for full details, see
3.8
.

To prove
1.2
we show that
\mathcal{T}
is quasi-isomorphic to a more homological
DG
-algebra. The complex
\mathrm{P}
has cohomology in two degrees, but regardless, it is just a bounded complex of projectives, and so we can consider its endomorphism
DG
-algebra
\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P})
.

Theorem 1.3

(
3.6
)
.

There is a natural morphism
\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P})\to\mathcal{T}
, which is a quasi-isomorphism of
DG
-algebras.

On one hand, this means we can interpret
\mathcal{T}
simply as a more explicit
DG
model of the more homological
\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P})
. On the other hand, the naturality of the definition of
\mathcal{T}
gives some motivation to then consider the slightly less motivated algebra
\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P})
.

1.2.
The unitally positive algebra

This subsection introduces
\mathcal{N}
, the unitally positive
A_{\infty}
-algebra. This object can be associated to (almost) any
DG
-category, but for simplicity this introduction restricts to the case of
DG
-algebras.

The motivation is simple: given a
DG
-algebra
\mathcal{B}
, it is well known that in general the good truncation
\uptau_{\geq 0}\mathcal{B}
need not be a
DG
-algebra. This is not the only issue. Regardless of whether it is a
DG
-algebra or not, in degree zero
\textnormal{H}^{0}(\uptau_{\geq 0}\mathcal{B})=\textnormal{H}^{0}(\mathcal{B})
. Often in examples (e.g.
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{T}
from the previous section) the cohomology in degree zero is a direct sum, and we only want to ‘access’ part of it.

For this reason, we now pass to the
A_{\infty}
-level, and consider a strictly unital minimal model of
\mathcal{B}
. It is elementary to see, in
5.5
, that this
A_{\infty}
-structure on the cohomology of
\mathcal{B}
restricts to give an
A_{\infty}
-structure on
\mathbb{C}\mathsf{1}\oplus\textnormal{H}^{>0}(\mathcal{B})
, where
\mathsf{1}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}[\operatorname{Id}_{\mathcal{B}}]\in\textnormal{H}^{0}(\mathcal{B})
.

Definition 1.4

(
5.6
)
.

Given a unital
DG
-algebra
\mathcal{B}
such that the unit is not a coboundary, we call the
A_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathbb{C}\mathsf{1}\oplus\textnormal{H}^{>0}(\mathcal{B})
, with
A_{\infty}
-structure induced from a strictly unital minimal model of
\mathcal{B}
(see
5.5
), the
unitally positive
A_{\infty}
-algebra
associated to
\mathcal{B}
.

Returning to the running setting of the periodic extension
DG
-algebra
\mathcal{T}
, at least in the case when
\textnormal{Hom}_{\Upgamma}(M,M)\cong\mathbb{C}
, the unitally positive
A_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{T}}
has the same cohomology as just the bottom row of
1.2
. Thus,
very
roughly speaking,
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{T}}
can be viewed as a ‘suitably positive’ truncation of the
DG
-algebra
\mathcal{T}
. Given it also contains the unit, this explains the name.

Of course, we show that in general the unitally positive
A_{\infty}
-algebra is well defined, up to quasi-isomorphism in the category of unital
DG
-algebras.

Proposition 1.5

(
5.7
)
.

If two unital
DG
-algebras
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{B}
are quasi-isomorphic as unital
DG
-algebras, there exists an
A_{\infty}
-quasi-isomorphism between
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{A}}
and
\mathcal{N}_{\mathcal{B}}
.

In fact, all constructions and results in this subsection extend to (almost) all
DG
-categories, and this extension is needed to cover the multi-curve version of the application in the next section; the reader is referred to §
8.1
for full details.

1.3.
Application: The Donovan–Wemyss conjecture

Recall that to every
3
-fold flopping contraction
X\to\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits R
, one can associate a finite dimensional noncommutative algebra
\mathrm{A}_{\textnormal{con}}
, known as the contraction algebra
[
DW1
,
DW3
]
. In this introduction we will restrict to the single-curve case, where it is well-known that
\mathrm{A}_{\textnormal{con}}
is local and the unique simple
\mathrm{A}_{\textnormal{con}}
-module
S
admits a length four complex
\mathrm{P}
of finitely generated projective
\mathrm{A}_{\textnormal{con}}
-modules which induce a periodic projective resolution; the multi-curve case is also solved in this paper, but details are left to §
8.3
.

Applying the above §
1.1
and §
1.2
to
S\in\mathop{\textnormal{mod}}\nolimits\mathrm{A}_{\textnormal{con}}
constructs the unitally positive
A_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathcal{N}_{S}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathbb{C}\mathsf{1}\oplus\textnormal{H}^{>0}(\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\mathrm{A}_{\textnormal{con}}}(\mathrm{P}))
. In this flops setting we can also view
S
as a module over the corresponding noncommutative crepant resolution
\mathrm{A}
in (
7.1
), where it has a finite projective resolution
\mathcal{Q}\to S
. Our main technical result is the following.

Theorem 1.6

(
6.6
)
.

There is an
A_{\infty}
-quasi-isomorphism between
\mathcal{N}_{S}
and
\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\mathrm{A}}(\mathcal{Q})
.

Roughly speaking, the point is that the
3
-CY object
\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\mathrm{A}}(\mathcal{Q})
can be reconstructed from the finite dimensional algebra
\mathrm{A}_{\textnormal{con}}
, via considering its simple module
S
and its associated unitally positive
A_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathcal{N}_{S}
. The precise statement is
6.7
.

The following is the main consequence, which reduces the classification of
3
-fold flops in birational geometry to that of finite dimensional algebras.

Corollary 1.7

(
7.2
, Donovan–Wemyss conjecture)
.

Suppose that
X_{1}\to\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits R_{1}
and
X_{2}\to\mathop{\rm Spec}\nolimits R_{2}
are two
3
-fold flopping contractions, where both
X_{i}
are smooth, both
R_{i}
are complete local, and both contractions have precisely one curve above the origin. Write
\mathrm{A}_{\textnormal{con}}
and
\mathrm{B}_{\textnormal{con}}
for their corresponding contraction algebras. Then

R_{1}\cong R_{2}\iff\mathrm{A}_{\textnormal{con}}\cong\mathrm{B}_{\textnormal{con}}.

The multi-curve version of
1.7
is also proved, in
8.13
. In either case, the point is that it is an immediate consequence of
1.6
and Koszul duality that, in this smooth setting, the contraction algebra
\mathrm{A}_{\textnormal{con}}
recovers its derived/DG version. This last statement is what originally motivated this paper: the construction of
\mathcal{N}_{S}
and then
1.6
should be viewed simply as the very direct way to achieve it. With this result in hand, both
1.7
and its multi-curve analogue
8.13
then follow quickly, since by
[
B
,
HK
]
, building on work of Kalck–Yang
[
KY
]
and others, it is well-known that the derived contraction algebra determines the isomorphism class of
R
.

Relation to Literature

This paper gives the second proof of
1.7
; the first was
[
JM
]
. The main benefits here are (a) the directness of the proof, and (b) the fact that the constructions made along the way are general. Indeed, perhaps the most remarkable aspect is that aside from both papers proving
1.7
, there is no technical overlap in either the approaches or in the settings considered. Consequently, there is no overlap in any of the results that lead to
1.7
. The paper
[
JM
]
restricts to a cluster tilting setting, and proves very strong bijection results in that particular setting; here the setting is broadly much more general (e.g.
\mathcal{T}
requires only a single periodic module, not a periodic algebra, and the construction of
\mathcal{N}
is very general indeed), and so the results here necessarily have a different flavour, given that bijection results do not hold in greater generality.

That said, it does seem notable that neither of the two approaches to
1.7
can prove the conjecture whilst staying entirely within the world of
DG
-algebras and categories. Both need to pass to the
A_{\infty}
-level in some fundamental way, in order to access structure which is currently not visible within the
DG
-world.

Versions of the unitally positive
A_{\infty}
-algebra
\mathcal{N}
have appeared implicitly in the literature in various settings, albeit (to the best of our knowledge) always applied to
DG
-endomorphism rings of objects in particular abelian categories, which automatically have no negative Ext groups. One example can be found in highest weight categories, where the ‘box algebra’ associated to the direct sum of the standard modules can be interpreted as an
\mathcal{N}
as constructed here; see e.g.
[
K3
, under 4.35]
and the references therein to
[
O
,
KKO
]
. A similar construction, in deformation theory, appears in the talks of Bodzenta–Bondal. Neither applies directly to our setting: the general construction of
\mathcal{N}
in
5.6
below applies to (almost) any
DG
-algebra, and the novelty in this paper is to use this generality to both remove negative cohomology, and also to tweak the degree zero part.

Conventions

Throughout, script font means that the object should be considered together with its
DG
or
A_{\infty}
structure, whichever is clear from the context. So,
\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits(P)
and
\mathop{\mathcal{H}\rm om}\nolimits
are graded over
\mathbb{Z}
. All
DG
-algebras
\mathcal{A}
will be unital, such that the identity is not a coboundary. We will denote
A_{\infty}
-morphisms and functors by
\mathcal{A}\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{B}
.

We will always draw complexes with arrows pointing to the right, namely

x\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\ldots\to a\to b\to c\to\ldots

Irrespective of how the objects in the complex
x
are notated or numbered,
\textnormal{H}^{-1}(x)
will always be the cohomology immediately to the left of the zero position. So, if
b
is in degree zero above,
\textnormal{H}^{-1}(x)
is the cohomology at
a
. Write
[1]
for the shift to the left, with a sign on the differential. Thus

x[1]\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\ldots\xrightarrow{-\updelta}b\xrightarrow{-\updelta}c\xrightarrow{-\updelta}d\to\ldots

where
c
is now in degree zero. Given an ring
\Upgamma
, modules will be right modules. We write
\circ f
for precomposition with the map
f
, i.e.,
g\mapsto g\circ f
, and write
f\circ
for postcomposition with
f
, i.e.,
g\mapsto f\circ g
.

Open Access

For the purpose of open access, the authors have applied a Creative Commons Attribution (CC:BY) licence to any Author Accepted Manuscript version arising from this submission.

2.
Trivial extension DG-algebras

Given any algebra
\Upgamma
, and any
\Upgamma
-module that admits a periodic projective resolution (in a rather weak sense), this section constructs an associated
trivial extension
DG
-algebra
. In particular, this setup applies to
\Upgamma
itself.

2.1.
Generalities

Throughout, let
\Upgamma
be a
k
-algebra, where
k
is some field.

Setup 2.1
.

Suppose that
M\in\mathop{\textnormal{mod}}\nolimits\Upgamma
admits a length
n\geq 1
periodic projective resolution, in the sense that there exists a complex of finitely generated projective
\Upgamma
-modules

\mathrm{P}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\quad 0\to P_{n-1}\xrightarrow{d_{n-1}}P_{n-2}\xrightarrow{d_{n-2}}\dots\xrightarrow{d_{2}}P_{1}\xrightarrow{d_{1}}P_{0}\to 0

such that

0\to M\xrightarrow{\upbeta}P_{n-1}\to P_{n-2}\to\dots\to P_{1}\to P_{0}\xrightarrow{\upalpha}M\to 0

is exact. Mildly abusing notation, by shifting the complex
\mathrm{P}
, and using the connecting map
d_{0}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\upbeta\circ\upalpha
, we construct the complex

\mathcal{P}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\quad\dots\to\mathrm{P}[2n]\xrightarrow{d_{0}[n]}\mathrm{P}[n]\xrightarrow{d_{0}}\mathrm{P}\to 0

where each component
\mathrm{P}[\ell n]
represents a length
n
complex of projective
\Upgamma
-modules. For calibration, the complex
\mathcal{P}
is written explicitly in the cases
n=1,2,3
in
2.2
below.

From this, consider the
DG
-algebra

\mathcal{E}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathcal{P})

in its usual graded pieces
\mathcal{E}=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\,\mathcal{E}^{i}
, where for calibration
\mathcal{E}^{1}
is the collection of all maps that point one position to the right

0
P_{0}
P_{1}
P_{2}
P_{3}
\ldots
P_{0}
P_{1}
\ldots
0
P_{0}
P_{1}
P_{2}
\ldots
P_{n-1}
P_{0}
P_{1}
\ldots

and there are no commutativity (or other) conditions on this collection. We will write
\mathcal{E}=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\,\mathcal{E}^{i}
with grading
|\,|
, multiplication
\circ
, and differential
\updelta
(see e.g.
[
K2
, §1.1]
).

The
DG
-algebra
\mathcal{E}
depends on both
\Upgamma
and
M
, but this is suppressed from the notation. We also remark that it is well-known that different choices of projective resolutions produce quasi-isomorphic
DG
-algebras
\mathcal{E}
.

Implicit in the above setup is the convention that shifting the complex
\mathrm{P}
by an odd homological degree introduces a minus sign on the differentials. Thus, when
n
is odd, some minus signs occur.

Example 2.2
.

For
n=1
the complex
\mathcal{P}
is

\dots\xrightarrow{\upbeta\circ\upalpha}P_{0}\xrightarrow{-\upbeta\circ\upalpha}P_{0}\xrightarrow{\upbeta\circ\upalpha}P_{0}\to 0.

For
n=2
the complex
\mathcal{P}
is

\dots\to P_{1}\xrightarrow{d_{1}}P_{0}\xrightarrow{\upbeta\circ\upalpha}P_{1}\xrightarrow{d_{1}}P_{0}\xrightarrow{\upbeta\circ\upalpha}P_{1}\xrightarrow{d_{1}}P_{0}\to 0,

and for
n=3
it is

\dots\xrightarrow{d_{2}}P_{1}\xrightarrow{d_{1}}P_{0}\xrightarrow{-\upbeta\circ\upalpha}P_{2}\xrightarrow{-d_{2}}P_{1}\xrightarrow{-d_{1}}P_{0}\xrightarrow{\upbeta\circ\upalpha}P_{2}\xrightarrow{d_{2}}P_{1}\xrightarrow{d_{1}}P_{0}\to 0.

Example 2.3
.

One module that always has such a periodic resolution (for any
n\geq 2
) is the projective module
\Upgamma
itself: we can take the complex

\mathrm{P}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\quad 0\to\Upgamma\to 0\to\dots\to 0\to\Upgamma\to 0

with
\upalpha=\upbeta=\operatorname{Id}
.

.

Note that
|\upsigma\kern 1.0pt|=n
and
|\uptau\kern 1.0pt|=-n
. It is a straightforward calculation to see that

\upsigma\circ\uptau=\operatorname{Id}\quad\text{ and}\quad\uptau\circ\upsigma=\operatorname{Id}_{\geq n}

(2.A)

where
\operatorname{Id}=\operatorname{Id}_{\mathcal{E}}
and
\operatorname{Id}_{\geq n}
denotes the degree 0 map obtained as the truncation of
\operatorname{Id}
that restricts to the identity on the components
\mathrm{P}[\ell n]
for all
\ell\geq 1
and the zero map on the component
\mathrm{P}
.

Lemma 2.5
.

Under Setup
2.1
,
\updelta(\upsigma)=0
.

Proof.

This is an easy verification. In components, either
\upsigma=\operatorname{Id}\colon P_{i}[\ell n]\rightarrow P_{i}[(\ell-1)n]
with
\ell>0
, or
\upsigma=0
, so we just need to check that
\updelta(\upsigma)=0
on the components
P_{i}[\ell n]
of
\mathcal{P}
with
\ell>0
. When
i\neq 0
, for any
\ell>0
we have

\displaystyle\updelta(\upsigma)

\displaystyle=d_{i}[(\ell-1)n]\circ\operatorname{Id}-(-1)^{|\upsigma|}\operatorname{Id}\circ\,d_{i}[\ell n]

\displaystyle=(-1)^{(\ell-1)n}d_{i}-(-1)^{n+\ell n}d_{i}

(
|\upsigma|=n
)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\ell n+n}d_{i}-(-1)^{n+\ell n}d_{i}

(
(-1)^{n}=(-1)^{-n}
)

\displaystyle=0.

Similarly, for
i=0
with
\ell>1
then

\displaystyle\updelta(\upsigma)

\displaystyle=(\upbeta\circ\upalpha)[(\ell-1)n]\circ\operatorname{Id}-(-1)^{|\upsigma|}\operatorname{Id}\circ(\upbeta\circ\upalpha)[\ell n]

\displaystyle=(-1)^{(\ell-1)n}\upbeta\circ\upalpha-(-1)^{n+\ell n}\upbeta\circ\upalpha

\displaystyle=0,

and for
i=0
with
\ell=1
, we have
\updelta(\upsigma)=0\circ\operatorname{Id}-(-1)^{|\upsigma|}\,0\circ(\upalpha\circ\upbeta)[n]=0
.
∎

2.2.
The trivial extension
DG
-algebra

The construction of the trivial extension
DG
-algebra involves isolating two periodic pieces of
\mathcal{E}
, the first of which is the following.

Definition 2.6
.

Under Setup
2.1
, write
{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}^{i}
, where

{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}^{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{x\in\mathcal{E}^{i}\mid\upsigma\circ x=(-1)^{|\upsigma||x|}x\circ\upsigma\}.

Lemma 2.7
.

With notation as above, the following hold.

(1)

If
x\in{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
is homogeneous, then
\upsigma\circ\updelta(x)=(-1)^{|\upsigma|(|x|+1)}\updelta(x)\circ\upsigma
.

(2)

{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
is a
DG
-subalgebra of
\mathcal{E}
.

Proof.

(1) On one hand, by the graded Leibniz rule

\updelta(\upsigma\circ x)=\updelta(\upsigma)\circ x+(-1)^{|\upsigma|}\upsigma\circ\updelta(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\ref{Lemma: deltasigma is zero}}}{{=}}(-1)^{|\upsigma|}\upsigma\circ\updelta(x).

On the other hand,

\displaystyle\updelta(\upsigma\circ x)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\upsigma||x|}\updelta(x\circ\upsigma)

(since
x\in{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\upsigma||x|}\big(\updelta(x)\circ\upsigma+(-1)^{|x|}x\circ\updelta(\upsigma)\big)

(graded Leibniz rule)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\upsigma||x|}\updelta(x)\circ\upsigma.

(
\updelta(\upsigma)=0
by
2.5
)

Equating terms and multiplying by
(-1)^{|\upsigma|}
gives
\upsigma\circ\updelta(x)=(-1)^{|\upsigma|(|x|+1)}\updelta(x)\circ\upsigma
.

(2) Clearly
\operatorname{Id}\in{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
, and further
{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
is a graded subspace of
\mathcal{E}
. It is closed under multiplication since for homogeneous
x,y\in{{}^{\upsigma}\mathcal{E}}

\upsigma\circ(x\circ y)=(-1)^{|\upsigma||x|}x\circ\upsigma\circ y=(-1)^{|\upsigma|(|x|+|y|)}(x\circ y)\circ\upsigma.

The differential
\updelta
on
\mathcal{E}
restricts to a differential on
{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
since for homogeneous
x\in{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
, by part (1)
\upsigma\circ\updelta(x)=(-1)^{|\upsigma|(|x|+1)}\updelta(x)\circ\upsigma=(-1)^{|\upsigma||\updelta(x)|}\updelta(x)\circ\upsigma
, and hence
\updelta(x)\in{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
.
∎

The second periodic piece of the trivial extension algebra is the following, which is very similar to the above, up to a sign.

Definition 2.8
.

Under Setup
2.1
, write
{{}^{{\scriptstyle-}\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}{{}^{{\scriptstyle-}\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}^{i}
, where

{{}^{{\scriptstyle-}\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}^{i}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\{y\in\mathcal{E}^{i}\mid\upsigma\circ y=(-1)^{|\upsigma|(|y|+1)}y\circ\upsigma\}

The following is the analogous version of
2.7
. Recall that a
DG

{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
-module is a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
-module endowed with a compatible differential
[
K2
, p1]
.

Lemma 2.9
.

With notation as above, the following hold.

(1)

If
y\in{{}^{{\scriptstyle-}\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
is homogeneous, then
\upsigma\circ\updelta(y)=(-1)^{|\upsigma||y|}\updelta(y)\circ\upsigma
.

(2)

{{}^{{\scriptstyle-}\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
is a
DG

{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
-bimodule.

Proof.

(1) The proof follows in a very similar manner to
2.7
(
1
).

(2) We first claim that
{{}^{{\scriptstyle-}\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
-module, on both the left and right. This holds since it is clear that
{{}^{{\scriptstyle-}\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
is a graded vector subspace of
\mathcal{E}
, and further
{{}^{{\scriptstyle-}\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
is closed under left and right multiplication by
{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
, since
for homogeneous
x\in{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
and
y\in{{}^{{\scriptstyle-}\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}

\displaystyle\upsigma\circ x\circ y

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\upsigma|(|x|+|y|+1)}x\circ y\circ\upsigma,

\displaystyle\upsigma\circ y\circ x

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\upsigma|(|x|+|y|+1)}y\circ x\circ\upsigma.

This makes
{{}^{{\scriptstyle-}\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded right and left
{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
-module. The bimodule compatibility condition for the left and right action,
a\circ(m\circ b)=(a\circ m)\circ b
for
a,b\in{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
and
m\in{{}^{{\scriptstyle-}\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
, holds since the actions are those inherited from the associative multiplication in
\mathcal{E}
.

Now the differential on
\mathcal{E}
restricts to a differential on
{{}^{{\scriptstyle-}\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
since for homogeneous
y\in{{}^{{\scriptstyle-}\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
, by (1),
\upsigma\circ\updelta(y)=(-1)^{|\upsigma||y|}\updelta(y)\circ\upsigma=(-1)^{|\upsigma|(|\updelta(y)|+1)}\updelta(y)\circ\upsigma
, and so
\updelta(y)\in{{}^{{\scriptstyle-}\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
. This differential is compatible with both the left and right action, namely

\updelta(a\circ m\circ b)=\updelta(a)\circ m\circ b+(-1)^{|a|}a\circ\updelta(m)\circ b+(-1)^{|a|+|m|}a\circ m\circ\updelta(b)

holds for
a\in{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}^{i}
,
m\in{{}^{{\scriptstyle-}\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}^{j}
, and
b\in{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
, since all differentials are inherited from
\mathcal{E}
, which obeys the Leibniz rule.
∎

Remark 2.10
.

If
|\upsigma|
is even,
{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}={{}^{{\scriptstyle-}\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
. In this case,
{{}^{{\scriptstyle-}\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
is clearly a
DG

{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
-bimodule.

The following is our main new object.

Definition 2.11
.

Under the Setup
2.1
, the trivial extension
DG
-algebra
\mathcal{T}
of
M\in\mathop{\textnormal{mod}}\nolimits\Upgamma
is defined as follows. As a graded vector space,

\mathcal{T}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}\oplus{{}^{{\scriptstyle-}\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}[1-n].

To avoid confusion on signs associated with the shift, we will always view a homogenous element of
\mathcal{T}
of degree
j
to be a pair
(x,y)
where
x\in{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}^{j}
and
y\in{{}^{{\scriptstyle-}\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}^{j-n+1}
are homogeneous elements of degrees
|x|=j
and
|y|=j+1-n
respectively; equivalently
j=|x|=|y|+n-1
. Thus, in this convention both
x
and
y
will be viewed in the fixed
\mathcal{E}
, and so the fixed differential
\updelta_{\mathcal{E}}=\updelta
can be applied to both.

Multiplication is defined on homogeneous elements by

(x,y)\cdot(a,b)=\big(x\circ a,\,x\circ b+(-1)^{|a|(|\upsigma|+1)}y\circ a\big),

and extended linearly. The differential
\upxi
on
\mathcal{T}
is defined on homogeneous elements by

\upxi(x,y)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\big(\updelta(x)-(-1)^{|x|}y\circ\upsigma,\,\updelta(y)\big)

and extended linearly.

A priori,
\mathcal{T}
depends on
\mathrm{P}
,
\upalpha
and
\upbeta
, but this is suppressed from the notation. As the above multiplication is a graded version of the multiplication of the usual trivial extension algebra, this partially justifies the name.

Remark 2.12
.

The differential
\upxi
is just the differential of the cone of the degree
n
morphism
{{}^{{\scriptstyle-}\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}\to{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
sending
y
to
(-1)^{|\upsigma|}y\circ\upsigma
and as such, it is very natural to consider. Here, instead of the usual sign convention on the differential of the cone of
f\colon V\to W
, which is
d_{C}(x,y)=(d_{W}x+f(y),-d_{V}y)
, for convenience and comparison with
\upxi
we are using the alternative convention that
d^{\prime}_{C}(x,y)=(d_{W}x+(-1)^{|y|}f(y),d_{V}y)
.

Proposition 2.13
.

Under the Setup
2.1
,
(\mathcal{T},\cdot,\upxi)
is a
DG
-algebra.

Proof.

We first check that multiplication is well-defined, namely that the two components remain in
{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}\oplus{{}^{{\scriptstyle-}\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}[1-n]
under multiplication. This is clear in the first component, and in the second component this follows since

\displaystyle\hskip-30.00005pt\upsigma\circ(x\circ b+(-1)^{|a|(|\upsigma|+1)}y\circ a)

\displaystyle=\upsigma\circ x\circ b+(-1)^{|a|(|\upsigma|+1)}\upsigma\circ y\circ a

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\upsigma||x|}x\circ\upsigma\circ b+(-1)^{|a|(|\upsigma|+1)+|\upsigma|(|y|+1)}y\circ\upsigma\circ a

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\upsigma|(|x|+|b|+1)}x\circ b\circ\upsigma+(-1)^{|a|(|\upsigma|+1)+|\upsigma|(|y|+|a|+1)}y\circ a\circ\upsigma

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\upsigma|(|x|+|b|+1)}(x\circ b+(-1)^{|a|(|\upsigma|+1)}y\circ a)\circ\upsigma

as
|x|+|b|=|y|+|a|
.

Now
\mathcal{T}
is clearly a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded vector space, with a multiplication that is compatible with the grading. To check that
\mathcal{T}
forms an algebra, we note that
(\operatorname{Id},0)\in\mathcal{T}
acts as the identity element, and associativity follows from the calculation

\displaystyle\hskip-30.00005pt\big((a,b)\cdot(c,d)\big)\cdot(e,f)

\displaystyle=\big(a\circ c,a\circ d+(-1)^{(|\upsigma|+1)|c|}b\circ c\big)\cdot(e,f)

\displaystyle=(a\circ c\circ e,a\circ c\circ f+(-1)^{(|\upsigma|+1)|e|}a\circ d\circ e+(-1)^{(|\upsigma|+1)(|c|+|e|)}b\circ c\circ e)

\displaystyle=(a,b)\cdot\big(c\circ e,c\circ f+(-1)^{(|\upsigma|+1)|e|}d\circ e\big)

\displaystyle=(a,b)\cdot\big((c,d)\cdot(e,f)\big).

Multiplication distributes over addition by definition, and thus
\mathcal{T}
is an algebra.

We next claim that
\upxi
is a differential. First, we claim that it is well defined on the level of sets. For homogeneous
(x,y)\in\mathcal{T}
, we know from
2.7
that
\updelta(x)\in{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
, and from
2.9
that
\updelta(y)\in{{}^{{\scriptstyle-}\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
. Also,
|y\circ\upsigma|=|y|+n=|x|+1=|\updelta(x)|
with

\displaystyle\upsigma\circ(y\circ\upsigma)

\displaystyle=\upsigma\circ y\circ\upsigma

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\upsigma|(|y|+1)}y\circ\upsigma\circ\upsigma

(
y\in{{}^{{\scriptstyle-}\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\upsigma|(|x|+|\upsigma|)}(y\circ\upsigma)\circ\upsigma

(
|y|+1\equiv_{2}|x|+|\upsigma|
)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\upsigma|(|x|+1)}(y\circ\upsigma)\circ\upsigma.

(
|\upsigma|^{2}\equiv_{2}|\upsigma|
)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\updelta(x)||\upsigma|}(y\circ\upsigma)\circ\upsigma.

Combining,
\upxi(x,y)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\big(\updelta(x)-(-1)^{|x|}y\circ\upsigma,\,\updelta(y)\big)\in{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}\oplus{{}^{{\scriptstyle-}\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
with the correct degree in the two factors, and so
\upxi
is well defined. It is clear that
\upxi
has degree one on
\mathcal{T}
.

It is easy see that
\upxi^{2}=0
, by just observing

\displaystyle\upxi^{2}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\upxi\left(\updelta(x)-(-1)^{|x|}y\circ\upsigma,\,\updelta(y)\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\updelta^{2}(x)-(-1)^{|x|}\updelta(y)\circ\upsigma-(-1)^{|\updelta(x)|}\updelta(y)\circ\upsigma,\,\updelta^{2}(y)\right)

\displaystyle=(0,0).

where we have used the graded Leibniz rule, and the fact that
\updelta(\upsigma)=0
by
2.5
. Thus
\upxi
is a well-defined differential.

To establish that
\mathcal{T}
is a
DG
-algebra, it remains to check that the graded Leibniz rule

\upxi\big((x,y)\cdot(a,b)\big)=\upxi(x,y)\cdot(a,b)+(-1)^{|x|}(x,y)\cdot\upxi(a,b)

is satisfied, where
(x,y)
and
(a,b)
are assumed to be homogeneous elements, so
|x|=|y|+n-1
and similarly
|a|=|b|+n-1
. On one hand,

\upxi\big((x,y)\cdot(a,b)\big)=\upxi\big(x\circ a,x\circ b+(-1)^{|a|(|\upsigma|+1)}y\circ a\big)=\\
\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\updelta(x)\circ a+(-1)^{|x|}x\circ\updelta(a)-(-1)^{|x|+|a|}x\circ b\circ\upsigma-(-1)^{|a|(|\upsigma|+1)+|a|+|x|}y\circ a\circ\upsigma,\\
\updelta(x)\circ b+(-1)^{|x|}x\circ\updelta(b)+(-1)^{|a|(|\upsigma|+1)}\updelta(y)\circ a+(-1)^{|a|(|\upsigma|+1)+|y|}y\circ\updelta(a)\end{array}\right).

On the other hand,

\upxi(x,y)\cdot(a,b)+(-1)^{|x|}(x,y)\cdot\upxi(a,b)=\\
\left(\updelta(x)-(-1)^{|x|}y\circ\upsigma,\updelta(y)\right)\cdot(a,b)+(-1)^{|x|}(x,y)\cdot\left(\updelta(a)-(-1)^{|a|}b\circ\upsigma,\updelta(b)\right).

Displaying the first and second component separately, we see that the first component is

\updelta(x)\circ a-(-1)^{|x|}y\circ\upsigma\circ a+(-1)^{|x|}x\circ\updelta(a)-(-1)^{|x|+|a|}x\circ b\circ\upsigma

and the second component is

\updelta(x)\circ b-(-1)^{|x|}y\circ\upsigma\circ b+(-1)^{|a|(|\upsigma|+1)}\updelta(y)\circ a+(-1)^{|x|}x\circ\updelta(b)\\
+(-1)^{|x|+|\updelta(a)|(|\upsigma|+1)}y\circ\updelta(a)-(-1)^{|x|+|a|+|\updelta(a)|(|\upsigma|+1)}y\circ b\circ\upsigma.

As such, to satisfy the graded Leibniz rule the required equality in the first component reduces to the identity

(-1)^{|a|(|\upsigma|+2)}y\circ a\circ\upsigma=(-1)^{|a||\upsigma|}y\circ a\circ\upsigma=y\circ\upsigma\circ a,

which is true since
a\in{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
. The required equality in the second component reduces to

(-1)^{|a|(|\upsigma|+1)+|y|}y\circ\updelta(a)=-(-1)^{|x|}y\circ\upsigma\circ b\\
+(-1)^{|x|+|\updelta(a)|(|\upsigma|+1)}y\circ\updelta(a)-(-1)^{|x|+|a|+|\updelta(a)|(|\upsigma|+1)}y\circ b\circ\upsigma.

which is true by the following two calculations. First,

\displaystyle(-1)^{|a|(|\upsigma|+1)+|y|}y\circ\updelta(a)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|a|(|\upsigma|+1)+|x|-|\upsigma|+1}y\circ\updelta(a)

(
|y|=|x|-|\upsigma|+1
)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{(|a|+1)(|\upsigma|+1)+|x|}y\circ\updelta(a)

(
|\upsigma|\equiv_{2}-|\upsigma|
)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|x|+|\updelta(a)|(|\upsigma|+1)}y\circ\updelta(a),

(
|\updelta(a)|=|a|+1
)

and second

\displaystyle y\circ\upsigma\circ b

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\upsigma|(|b|+1)}y\circ b\circ\upsigma

(
\upsigma\circ b=(-1)^{|\upsigma|(|b|+1)}b\circ\upsigma
)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\upsigma|(|a|+|\upsigma|)}y\circ b\circ\upsigma

(
|b|+1\equiv_{2}|a|+|\upsigma|
)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\upsigma|(|a|+1)}y\circ b\circ\upsigma

(
|\upsigma|^{2}\equiv_{2}|\upsigma|
)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{(|\upsigma|+1)(|a|+1)+|a|+1}y\circ b\circ\upsigma

\displaystyle=-(-1)^{|a|+|\updelta(a)|(|\upsigma|+1)}y\circ b\circ\upsigma.

(
|\updelta(a)|=|a|+1
)

Hence the graded Leibniz rule is satisfied, and
\mathcal{T}
is a
DG
-algebra.
∎

3.
Quasi-isomorphisms and cohomology

Under the running Setup
2.1
, in §
3.2
we show that the trivial extension
DG
-algebra
\mathcal{T}
is quasi-isomorphic, as unital
DG
-algebras, to
\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P})
. This then allows us in §
3.3
to compute the cohomology of
\mathcal{T}
, which it turns out has a very elegant description.

3.1.
Notation and preliminaries

With notation as in
2.1
, consider the inclusion
\upiota\colon\mathrm{P}\to\mathcal{P}
, namely

\mathrm{P}
\ldots
0
0
P_{n-1}
\ldots
P_{0}
0
\scriptstyle d_{n-1}
\scriptstyle d_{1}
\mathcal{P}
\ldots
P_{1}
P_{0}
P_{n-1}
\ldots
P_{0}
0
\scriptstyle(-1)^{n}d_{2}
\scriptstyle(-1)^{n}d_{1}
\scriptstyle\upbeta\circ\upalpha
\scriptstyle d_{n-1}
\scriptstyle d_{1}
\scriptstyle\upiota

where the rightmost
P_{0}
of the complex is in degree zero.

It is clear from the above diagram that
d_{\mathcal{P}}\circ\upiota=\upiota\circ d_{\mathrm{P}}
, so that
\upiota
induces an injective morphism of complexes of vector spaces

\upiota\circ\colon\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P})\to\mathop{\mathcal{H}\rm om}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P},\mathcal{P}),

so that

\upiota\circ\colon\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P})\xrightarrow{\sim}\{\upiota\circ g\mid g\in\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P})\}\subseteq\mathop{\mathcal{H}\rm om}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P},\mathcal{P})

(3.A)

is an isomorphism of complexes of vector spaces.

Notation 3.1
.

Consider the Setup
2.1
, and recall that
\mathcal{E}=\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathcal{P})
.

(1)

Given
x\in\mathcal{E}
, write
x_{0}\in\mathop{\mathcal{H}\rm om}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P},\mathcal{P})
for the map obtained as the restriction of
x\colon\mathcal{P}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}
to the first component
\mathrm{P}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}
.

(2)

For
f\in\mathop{\mathcal{H}\rm om}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P},\mathcal{P})
, write
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}
for the element of
\mathcal{E}
obtained by graded repetition. More precisely,

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\left((-1)^{|\ell n||f|}f_{\ell}\right)_{\ell=0}^{\infty}

where
f_{\ell}\colon\colon\mathrm{P}[\ell n]\rightarrow\mathcal{P}
is just
f
viewed out of
\mathrm{P}[\ell n]
instead of out of
\mathrm{P}
, which we can do since the codomain
\mathcal{P}
is periodic. If
|f|>0
then some of the components of
f
are maps
P_{i}\to 0
. These get repeated as the zero map, although their codomain under repetition is no longer
0
.
Visually,

since
|\upsigma|=n
. For
f\in\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P})
, likewise write
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}
for the element of
\mathcal{E}
obtained by graded repetition of
\upiota\circ f\in\mathop{\mathcal{H}\rm om}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P},\mathcal{P})
.

(3)

For
f\in\mathop{\mathcal{H}\rm om}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P},\mathcal{P})
or in
\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P})
, set
\Updelta(f)\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\updelta(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}\,)-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
.

The following is elementary, but important.

Lemma 3.2
.

With notation as above, if
x\in{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
, then
x_{0}
belongs to
\{\upiota\circ g\mid g\in\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P})\}\subseteq\mathop{\mathcal{H}\rm om}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P},\mathcal{P})
. By the isomorphism (
3.A
), we can then think of
x_{0}
as belonging to
\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P})
.

Proof.

Consider the equation
\upsigma\circ x=(-1)^{|\upsigma||x|}x\circ\upsigma
restricted to
\mathrm{P}
. Since
\upsigma
maps
\mathrm{P}
to zero,
x\circ\upsigma
is zero on
\mathrm{P}
, and it follows that
\upsigma\circ x
is zero on
\mathrm{P}
, so
\upsigma\circ x_{0}=0
. In particular, any of the non-zero maps which constitute
x_{0}
cannot land in
\mathrm{P}[\ell n]
with
\ell>0
, since then
\upsigma\circ x_{0}\neq 0
. The non-zero maps cannot land to the right of
\mathrm{P}
, since that is zero. Hence
x_{0}
is a collection of maps, and the non-zero ones necessarily take
\mathrm{P}
to
\mathrm{P}
. The claim follows.
∎

In the remainder of this subsection, working under Setup
2.1
, we will work towards building a quasi-isomorphism of
DG
-algebras

\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P})\to\mathcal{T}.

This requires the following easy, direct, verifications.

Lemma 3.3
.

For
f,g\in\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P})
, the following hold:

(1)

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}\circ\,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}
.

(2)

\Updelta(f\circ g)=\Updelta(f)\circ\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}+(-1)^{|f|}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}\circ\Updelta(g)
.

Proof.

For (1), since the complex
\mathrm{P}
has precisely
n
non-zero entries,
f
and
g
are encoded by a collection of
n
maps, say
(\uppsi_{i})
and
(\upvarphi_{i})
respectively, where
\uppsi_{i},\upvarphi_{i}
have domain
P_{i}
. Both
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}\circ\,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}
are encoded by an infinite number of maps, and we must show that the statement holds for each of these.

Choose an arbitrary
P_{j}
in the component
\mathrm{P}[\ell n]
, for some
0\leq j\leq n-1
and some
\ell\geq 0
. If
\upvarphi_{j}=0
then
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}\circ\,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}
out of this
P_{j}
, since both are zero. Hence we can assume that
\upvarphi_{j}\neq 0
, and so since
\mathrm{P}
has length
n
, necessarily we can write
\upvarphi_{j}\colon P_{j}\to P_{k}
for some
0\leq k\leq n-1
. It follows that
(-1)^{|\ell n||g|}\upvarphi_{j}
maps
P_{j}
(in the component
\mathrm{P}[\ell n]
) into the same component
\mathrm{P}[\ell n]
of
\mathcal{P}
. Thus, under composition, the sign on
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}
is
(-1)^{|\ell n||f|}
, and so overall on this
P_{j}
we have

\displaystyle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}\circ\,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\ell n|(|f|+|g|)}\,\uppsi_{k}\circ\upvarphi_{j}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}.

The result follows.

For (2),

\displaystyle\Updelta(f\circ g)

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\updelta\big(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}\big)-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}

\displaystyle=\updelta\big(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}\circ\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}\big)-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}

(Part (
1
))

\displaystyle=\updelta\big(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}\big)\circ\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}+(-1)^{|f|}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}\circ\updelta\big(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}\big)-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}

(graded Leibniz, twice)

\displaystyle=\updelta\big(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}\big)\circ\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}+(-1)^{|f|}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}\circ\updelta\big(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}\big)-\big(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\circ\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}+(-1)^{|f|}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}\circ\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\big)

(Part (
1
))

\displaystyle=\big(\updelta(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f})-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\big)\circ\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}+(-1)^{|f|}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}\circ\big(\updelta(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\big)

\displaystyle=\Updelta(f)\circ\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}+(-1)^{|f|}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}\circ\Updelta(g).

∎

Lemma 3.4
.

Given
x\in\mathcal{E}
,
g\in\mathop{\mathcal{H}\rm om}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P},\mathcal{P})
, the following hold.

(1)

If
x=y\circ\upsigma
for some
y\in\mathcal{E}
, then
x_{0}=(y\circ\upsigma)_{0}=0
.

(2)

Restriction commutes with the differentials, namely
\updelta(x_{0})=\big(\updelta(x)\big)_{0}
.

(3)

(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})_{0}=g
.

(4)

\uptau\circ\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}=(-1)^{|\upsigma||g|}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}\circ\uptau
.

(5)

\updelta(\Updelta(g))=-\Updelta(\updelta(g))
.

(6)

\Updelta(g)\circ\uptau\circ\upsigma=\Updelta(g)
.

(7)

If
x
satisfies
x\circ\uptau\circ\upsigma=x
(e.g.
x=\Updelta(g)
by
(6)
above), then
x\circ\updelta(\uptau)=0
.

(8)

If
x\in{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
and
x\circ\uptau\circ\upsigma=x
, then
x\circ\uptau\in{{}^{{\scriptstyle-}\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
.

Proof.

Part (1) holds since
\upsigma
maps
\mathrm{P}
to zero, hence
y\circ\upsigma
maps
\mathrm{P}
to zero. Parts (2) and (3) are immediate consequences of the definitions. For Part (4),
g
is encoded by finitely many morphisms
(\upvarphi_{i})_{i=0}^{n-1}
, where each
\upvarphi_{i}
has domain
P_{i}
. To show the statement, consider the complex
\mathcal{P}
, and choose an arbitrary
P_{j}
in the component
\mathrm{P}[\ell n]
, for some
0\leq j\leq n-1
and some
\ell\geq 0
. If
\upvarphi_{j}=0
, then
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}
restricted to all
P_{j}
is zero, hence
\uptau\circ\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}=0=(-1)^{|\upsigma||g|}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}\circ\uptau
holds on
P_{j}
. Else
\upvarphi_{j}\neq 0
, in which case
\upvarphi_{j}
lands in the non-zero part of the complex
\mathcal{P}
. As
\uptau
points to the left, the fact that
\uptau\circ\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}=(-1)^{|\upsigma||g|}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}\circ\uptau
holds on
P_{j}
is then clear.

Part (5) follows since
\updelta^{2}=0
, and so using this twice gives

\updelta\big(\Updelta(g)\big)=\updelta\big(\updelta(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\big)=-\updelta\big(\,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\,\big)=-\updelta\big(\,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\,\big)+\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}=-\Updelta(\updelta(g)).

Part (6) holds since by (
2.A
)
(\operatorname{Id}-\uptau\circ\upsigma)
is the identity on
\mathrm{P}
and is zero elsewhere, and furthermore
\updelta(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
agree on
\mathrm{P}
, since there they both equal
\updelta(g)
. Hence
\Updelta(g)\circ(\operatorname{Id}-\uptau\circ\upsigma)=0
. Part (7) is

\displaystyle x\circ\updelta(\uptau)

\displaystyle=x\circ\uptau\circ\upsigma\circ\updelta(\uptau)

(by assumption on
x
)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\upsigma|}x\circ\uptau\circ\updelta(\upsigma\circ\uptau)

(Leibniz Rule, and
\updelta(\upsigma)=0
by
2.5
)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\upsigma|}x\circ\uptau\circ\updelta(\operatorname{Id})

(
\upsigma\circ\uptau=\operatorname{Id}
by (
2.A
))

\displaystyle=0.

For Part (8), observe that

\displaystyle\upsigma\circ x\circ\uptau

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\upsigma||x|}x\circ\upsigma\circ\uptau

(
x\in{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
by assumption)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\upsigma||x|}x

(
\upsigma\circ\uptau=\operatorname{Id}
by (
2.A
))

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\upsigma|(|x|+|\uptau|+1)}x

(
|\upsigma||\uptau|\equiv_{2}-|\upsigma|
, since
-n^{2}\equiv_{2}-n
)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{|\upsigma|(|x|+|\uptau|+1)}x\circ\uptau\circ\upsigma,

(by assumption on
x
)

and so
x\circ\uptau\in{{}^{{\scriptstyle-}\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
.
∎

The above result holds for all
g\in\mathop{\mathcal{H}\rm om}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P},\mathcal{P})
. When
g\in\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P})
, we can say more.

Lemma 3.5
.

If
g\in\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P})
, then the following hold.

(1)

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}\in{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
.

(2)

\Updelta(g)\in{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
.

(3)

\Updelta(g)\circ\uptau\in{{}^{{\scriptstyle-}\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
.

Proof.

For Part (1), by
3.4
(
3
)
(\upsigma\circ\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})_{0}=\upsigma\circ(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})_{0}=\upsigma\circ g
, thus since
g
takes
\mathrm{P}
to
\mathrm{P}
and
\upsigma
points to the right,
(\upsigma\circ\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})_{0}=0
.
By
3.4
(
1
),
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}\circ\upsigma)_{0}=0
. Combining, we see that
\upsigma\circ\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}=(-1)^{|\upsigma||g|}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}\circ\upsigma
holds on
\mathrm{P}
, since both equal
0
.

Since
g\in\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P})
, the only non-zero morphisms that constitute
\upiota\circ g
take
\mathrm{P}
to
\mathrm{P}
, hence the only non-zero morphisms that constitute
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}
map components
\mathrm{P}[\ell n]
to themselves. We finally claim that
\upsigma\circ\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}=(-1)^{|\upsigma||g|}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}\circ\upsigma
holds on all components
\mathrm{P}[\ell n]
with
\ell>0
. Since the only non-zero morphisms that constitute
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}
map
\mathrm{P}[\ell n]
to itself,
\upsigma
points to the right, and
\ell>0
,
\upsigma\circ\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}=(-1)^{|\upsigma||g|}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}\circ\upsigma
follows.

For Part (2),
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}\in{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
by (1), so since
{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
is closed under the differential by
2.7
(
2
), necessarily
\updelta(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})\in{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
. Also,
\updelta(g)\in\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P})
, so applying (1) to
\updelta(g)
gives
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\in{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
. Combining,
\Updelta(g)=\updelta(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\in{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
.

Part (3) is just
3.4
(
8
), using (2) together with
3.4
(
6
) to see that the two assumptions on
x=\Updelta(g)
hold.
∎

3.2.
The quasi-isomorphism

The following is the main result of this subsection.

Theorem 3.6
.

Under Setup
2.1
and Notation
3.1
, the following are morphisms of
DG

\mathbb{Z}
-modules

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathcal{T}&\longleftrightarrow&\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P})\\
(x,y)&\mapsto&x_{0}\\
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g},\,(-1)^{|g|}\Updelta(g)\circ\uptau)&\mapsfrom&g\end{array}

which induce bijections on cohomology. Moreover, the bottom map is a morphism of
DG
-algebras.

Proof.

Write
\mathbb{F}
for the top map, and
\mathbb{G}
for the bottom map. The map
\mathbb{F}
is well defined since by
3.2
, the restiction
x_{0}
belongs to
\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P})
. The map
\mathbb{G}
is well defined since
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}\in{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
by
3.5
(
1
) and
(-1)^{|g|}\Updelta(g)\circ\uptau\in{{}^{{\scriptstyle-}\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
by
3.5
(
3
). Thus both
\mathbb{F}
and
\mathbb{G}
are well defined, and they are clearly morphisms of graded
\mathbb{Z}
-modues.

We now claim that
\mathbb{F}
and
\mathbb{G}
respect the differentials. This is true for
\mathbb{F}
since

\displaystyle\mathbb{F}(\upxi(x,y))=(\updelta(x)-(-1)^{|x|}y\circ\upsigma)_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\ref{prep for DG}\eqref{prep for DG 1}}}{{=}}\updelta(x)_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptstyle\ref{prep for DG}\eqref{prep for DG 2}}}{{=}}\updelta(x_{0})=\updelta\,\mathbb{F}(x,y),

and it is true for
\mathbb{G}
since

\displaystyle\upxi\,\mathbb{G}(g)

\displaystyle=\upxi(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g},(-1)^{|g|}\Updelta(g)\circ\uptau)

\displaystyle=\big(\updelta(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})-(-1)^{|g|+|g|}\Updelta(g)\circ\uptau\circ\upsigma,(-1)^{|g|}\updelta(\Updelta(g)\circ\uptau)\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\updelta(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})-\Updelta(g),(-1)^{|g|}\updelta(\Updelta(g))\circ\uptau+(-1)^{|g|+|g|+1}\Updelta(g)\circ\updelta(\uptau)\big)

(by
3.4
(
6
))

\displaystyle=\big(\updelta(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})-(\updelta(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}),(-1)^{|g|}\updelta(\Updelta(g))\circ\uptau\big)

(by
3.4
(
7
))

\displaystyle=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{},(-1)^{|\updelta(g)|}\Updelta(\updelta(g))\circ\uptau)

(by
3.4
(
5
))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{G}(\updelta(g)).

Next, we claim that
\mathbb{F}
and
\mathbb{G}
restrict to an inverse bijection on cohomology. It is clear that
\mathbb{F}(\mathbb{G}(g))=(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})_{0}=g
by
3.4
(
3
), and so we just need to establish that
\mathbb{G}\circ\mathbb{F}
is the identity when restricted to cohomology.

Now, any
x\in{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
can be written in the form

x=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{x}+(x-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{x})=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{x}+(x-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{x})\circ\operatorname{Id}_{\geq n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\scriptstyle\eqref{sig tau basic}}}}{{=}}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{x}+(x-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{x})\circ\uptau\circ\upsigma

since
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{x}
and
x
agree on
\mathrm{P}
. Hence
(x-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{x})\circ\uptau\circ\upsigma=(x-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{x})
, so by
3.4
(
7
),
(x-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{x})\circ\updelta(\uptau)=0
. Furthermore, as
x\in{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
by assumption,
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{x}\in{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
by combining
3.2
and
3.5
(
1
). Thus
(x-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{x})\in{{}^{\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
. Hence, by
3.4
(
8
) applied to
x-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{x}
, we deduce that
(x-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{x})\circ\uptau\in{{}^{{\scriptstyle-}\upsigma}\kern-1.0pt\mathcal{E}}
.

Hence
z\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}(0,(-1)^{|x|}(x-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{x})\circ\uptau)\in\mathcal{T}
is an element of degree
|x|-1
such that

\displaystyle\upxi(z)

\displaystyle=\left(-(-1)^{|x|-1}(-1)^{|x|}(x-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{x})\circ\uptau\circ\upsigma,(-1)^{|x|}\updelta((x-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{x})\circ\uptau)\right)

\displaystyle=\left((x-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{x}),(-1)^{|x|}\updelta(x-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{x})\circ\uptau+(-1)^{|x|+|x|}(x-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{x})\circ\updelta(\uptau)\right)

\displaystyle=\left((x-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{x}),(-1)^{|x|}\updelta(x-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{x})\circ\uptau\right)

\displaystyle=\left((x-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{x}),(-1)^{|x|}\updelta(x)\circ\uptau-(-1)^{|x|}\updelta(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{x})\circ\uptau\right).

Using this we then calculate

\displaystyle\left(x,(-1)^{|x|}\updelta(x)\circ\uptau\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{x},(-1)^{|x|}\updelta(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{x})\circ\uptau\right)+\upxi(z)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{G}(x_{0})+\upxi(z).

Now an element
(x,y)\in\mathcal{T}
belongs to the kernel of
\upxi
if and only if

\updelta(x)-(-1)^{|x|}y\circ\upsigma=0\quad\text{ and }\quad\updelta(y)=0.

Right multiplying the first identity by
\uptau
, and recalling that
\upsigma\circ\uptau=\operatorname{Id}
by (
2.A
), we deduce that for
(x,y)
to be in the kernel of
\upxi
it is necessary that
y=(-1)^{|x|}\updelta(x)\circ\uptau
. Hence if
(x,y)\in\mathop{\rm Ker}\nolimits\upxi
, then
(x,y)=(x,(-1)^{|x|}\updelta(x)\circ\uptau)
, so by the above

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}(\mathbb{F}(x,y))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{G}(x_{0})=(x,y)-\upxi(z).

As such,
\mathbb{G}
and
\mathbb{F}
induce a bijection on cohomology.

Finally, to show that
\mathbb{G}
is a morphism of
DG
-algebras, we just need to check it preserves the products. This follows since

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}(f)\cdot\mathbb{G}(g)

\displaystyle=\big(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f},(-1)^{|f|}\Updelta(f)\circ\uptau)\cdot(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g},(-1)^{|g|}\Updelta(g)\circ\uptau\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f},(-1)^{|g|}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}\circ\Updelta(g)\circ\uptau+(-1)^{|g|(|\upsigma|+1)+|f|}\Updelta(f)\circ\uptau\circ\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}\big)

(by definition of
\cdot
, and
3.3
(
1
))

\displaystyle=\big(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f},(-1)^{|g|}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}\circ\Updelta(g)\circ\uptau+(-1)^{|g|(|\upsigma|+1)+|f|+|\upsigma||g|}\Updelta(f)\circ\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}\circ\uptau\big)

(by
3.4
(
4
))

\displaystyle=\big(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f},(-1)^{|g|}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}\circ\Updelta(g)\circ\uptau+(-1)^{|f|+|g|}\Updelta(f)\circ\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}\circ\uptau\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f},(-1)^{(|f|+|g|)}(\Updelta(f)\circ\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}+(-1)^{|f|}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f}\circ\Updelta(g))\circ\uptau\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{f},(-1)^{(|f|+|g|)}\Updelta(f\circ g)\circ\uptau\big)

(by
3.3
(
2
))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{G}(f\circ g).\qed

3.3.
Computation of cohomology

In this section we compute the cohomology of the
DG
-algebra
\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P})
, and thus via
3.6
the cohomology of
\mathcal{T}
. Again, consider the complex
\mathrm{P}
of projectives, which induces an exact sequence

0\to M\xrightarrow{\upbeta}P_{n-1}\to P_{n-2}\to\dots\to P_{1}\to P_{0}\xrightarrow{\upalpha}M\to 0.

Using the connecting map
d_{0}\mathrel{\mathchoice{\vbox{\hbox{$\displaystyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle:$}}}{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle:$}}}{=}}\upbeta\circ\upalpha
, accounting for signs correctly as in
2.2
, we obtain the infinite projective resolution
\mathcal{P}
of the
{\Upgamma}
-module
M
.

As in §
3.1
, write
\upiota
for the inclusion
\upiota\colon\mathrm{P}\to\mathcal{P}
. This induces a short exact sequence of complexes

0\to\mathrm{P}\xrightarrow{\upiota}\mathcal{P}\xrightarrow{\uppi}\mathcal{P}_{\leq n}\to 0,

where the complex
\mathcal{P}_{\leq n}
is the brutal truncation of
\mathcal{P}
, as illustrated in the following diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{n-2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{n-2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{\leq n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ldots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\upiota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\uppi}

(3.B)

Notice that the construction of
\mathcal{P}
, in particular the sign choices spelled out in
2.2
, together with the fact that
[1]
shifts to the left with a sign, implies that there is an equality of complexes

\mathcal{P}_{\leq n}[-n]=\mathcal{P}.

(3.C)

Lemma 3.7
.

Given
M
,
\mathrm{P}
and
\mathcal{P}
as above, let
N
be a
\Upgamma
-module, and consider the map
\circ\uppi\colon\mathop{\mathcal{H}\rm om}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathcal{P}_{\leq n},N)\to\mathop{\mathcal{H}\rm om}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathcal{P},N)
.

(1)

The map

\textnormal{Hom}_{\Upgamma}(M,N)\to\textnormal{Ext}^{n}_{\Upgamma}(M,N)

induced by
\textnormal{H}^{n}(\circ\uppi)
is surjective, and has kernel
\operatorname{Im}(\circ d_{0})\subseteq\textnormal{Hom}_{\Upgamma}(P_{0},N)
.

(2)

For every
i>0
, the map

\textnormal{Ext}^{i}_{\Upgamma}(M,N)\to\textnormal{Ext}^{i+n}_{\Upgamma}(M,N)

induced by
\textnormal{H}^{i+n}(\circ\uppi)
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{P}
is a projective resolution of
M
, we can use the complex
\mathop{\mathcal{H}\rm om}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathcal{P},N)
to calculate the groups
\textnormal{Ext}^{i}_{\Upgamma}(M,N)
, since with our conventions on grading,

\textnormal{H}^{i}(\mathop{\mathcal{H}\rm om}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathcal{P},M))\cong\textnormal{Ext}^{i}(M,N).

By (
3.C
), we can also use
\mathop{\mathcal{H}\rm om}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathcal{P}_{\leq n},N)
to calculate the same (shifted) Ext groups, via
\textnormal{Ext}^{i}_{\Upgamma}(M,N)\cong\textnormal{H}^{i+n}(\mathop{\mathcal{H}\rm om}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathcal{P}_{\leq n},N))
.

Applying
\textnormal{Hom}_{\Upgamma}(-,N)
to the bottom half of (
3.B
) gives a commutative diagram

(3.D)

where
{}_{\Upgamma}(P_{k},N)
is shorthand for
\textnormal{Hom}_{\Upgamma}(P_{k},N)
, and
{}_{\Upgamma}(P_{0},N)
is in degree
n
. It is clear from (
3.D
) that
\textnormal{H}^{j}(\circ\uppi)
is the identity when
j>n
, and
\textnormal{H}^{j}(\circ\uppi)
is zero if
j<n
. In particular, if
i>0
, then
\textnormal{H}^{i+n}(\circ\uppi)=\mathrm{Id}
and so it induces morphisms

\textnormal{Ext}^{i}_{\Upgamma}(M,N)\cong\textnormal{H}^{i+n}(\mathop{\mathcal{H}\rm om}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathcal{P}_{\leq n},N))\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Id}}\textnormal{H}^{i+n}(\mathop{\mathcal{H}\rm om}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathcal{P},N))\cong\textnormal{Ext}^{i+n}_{\Upgamma}(M,N).

The composition is an isomorphism, proving (2).

For (1), notice from (
3.D
) that
\textnormal{H}^{n}(\circ\uppi)
is the induced map

\mathop{\rm Ker}\nolimits(\circ d_{1})\to\mathop{\rm Ker}\nolimits(\circ d_{1})/\operatorname{Im}(\circ d_{0}).

Since this is induced by the identity, it is clearly surjective with claimed kernel.
∎

Consider now the following commutative diagram, where all the rows and columns are exact, because
\mathrm{P},\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\leq n}
are bounded-above complexes of projective modules:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathop{\mathcal{H}\rm om}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathcal{P}_{\leq n},\mathrm{P})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathop{\mathcal{H}\rm om}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathcal{P},\mathrm{P})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathop{\mathcal{H}\rm om}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathcal{P}_{\leq n},\mathcal{P})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathcal{P})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathop{\mathcal{H}\rm om}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P},\mathcal{P})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathcal{P}_{\leq n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathop{\mathcal{H}\rm om}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}_{\leq n})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathop{\mathcal{H}\rm om}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P},\mathcal{P}_{\leq n})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ\uppi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\upiota\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ\upiota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\upiota\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\upiota\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ\uppi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\uppi\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ\upiota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\uppi\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\uppi\circ}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ\uppi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\circ\upiota}

This diagram will allow us to compute the cohomology of
\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P})
and of
\mathop{\mathcal{H}\rm om}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P},\mathcal{P})
. As in the proof of
3.7
, the map

\textnormal{Ext}^{i-n}_{\Upgamma}(M,M)\cong\textnormal{H}^{i}(\mathop{\mathcal{H}\rm om}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathcal{P}_{\leq n},\mathcal{P}))\to\textnormal{H}^{i}(\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathcal{P}))\cong\textnormal{Ext}^{i}_{\Upgamma}(M,M)

is an isomorphism for
i>n
, and is surjective for
i=n
, with kernel
\operatorname{Im}(\circ d_{0})\subseteq\textnormal{Hom}_{\Upgamma}(P_{0},M)
.
Likewise, the map

\textnormal{Ext}^{i}_{\Upgamma}(M,M)\cong\textnormal{H}^{i}(\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathcal{P}_{\leq n}))\to\textnormal{H}^{i}(\mathop{\mathcal{H}\rm om}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P}_{\leq n}))\cong\textnormal{Ext}^{i+n}_{\Upgamma}(M,M)

is an isomorphism for
i>0
, and is surjective for
i=0
, with kernel
\operatorname{Im}(\circ d_{0})\subseteq\textnormal{Hom}_{\Upgamma}(P_{0},M)
.

Notice that we cannot obtain the same kind of information for the map induced by
\circ\uppi
in the first row of the diagram, because the complexes in the first row do not calculate Ext groups, since
\mathrm{P}
is not a projective resolution. Therefore, to calculate the underlying graded vector space of the cohomology of
\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P})
, we proceed by calculating the cohomology of the complexes in the last column in the second and third rows, using the information we know about the maps induced by
\circ\uppi
in the second and third row.
Thus we obtain from the second row an isomorphism of vector spaces

\textnormal{H}^{i}(\mathop{\mathcal{H}\rm om}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P},\mathcal{P}))=\begin{cases}0&\quad i<0,\ i\geq n\\
\textnormal{Ext}^{i}_{\Upgamma}(M,M)&\quad i=0,\ldots,n-2\\
\textnormal{Ext}^{n-1}_{\Upgamma}(M,M)\oplus\operatorname{Im}(\circ d_{0})&\quad i=n-1,\end{cases}

(3.E)

and from the third row an isomorphism of vector spaces

\textnormal{H}^{i}(\mathop{\mathcal{H}\rm om}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P},\mathcal{P}_{\leq n}))=\begin{cases}0&\quad i<-n,\ i\geq 0\\
\textnormal{Ext}^{i+n}_{\Upgamma}(M,M)&\quad i=-n,\ldots,-2\\
\textnormal{Ext}^{n-1}_{\Upgamma}(M,M)\oplus\operatorname{Im}(\circ d_{0})&\quad i=-1.\end{cases}

(3.F)

From these, using the third column in the diagram above, we can calculate the cohomology of
\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P})
, which, by
3.6
, coincides with the cohomology of
\mathcal{T}
. In fact, from the third column one obtains a long exact sequence in cohomology relating
\textnormal{H}^{*}(\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P}))
, (
3.E
) and (
3.F
). From the form of (
3.E
) and (
3.F
), one notices that the map induced by
\uppi\circ
in cohomology is always zero, because either the source or the target is zero. This immediately implies the following.

Theorem 3.8
.

The only non-zero cohomology groups of the
DG
-algebras
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P})
are the following, where in each degree we take the direct sum of the vector spaces stated.

\textnormal{H}^{-n+1}(\mathcal{T})

\ldots

\textnormal{H}^{-1}(\mathcal{T})

\textnormal{H}^{0}(\mathcal{T})

\textnormal{H}^{1}(\mathcal{T})

\ldots

\textnormal{H}^{n-2}(\mathcal{T})

\textnormal{H}^{n-1}(\mathcal{T})

\textnormal{Hom}_{\Upgamma}(M,M)

\ldots

\textnormal{Ext}^{n-2}_{\Upgamma}(M,M)

\textnormal{Ext}^{n-1}_{\Upgamma}(M,M)

\operatorname{Im}(\circ d_{0})

\textnormal{Hom}_{\Upgamma}(M,M)

\textnormal{Ext}^{1}_{\Upgamma}(M,M)

\ldots

\textnormal{Ext}^{n-2}_{\Upgamma}(M,M)

\textnormal{Ext}^{n-1}_{\Upgamma}(M,M)

\operatorname{Im}(\circ d_{0})

4.
Yoneda and Uniqueness

This section is concerned with basic properties of periodic resolutions, in terms of Yoneda extension classes, and from this answers questions on the dependence of the
DG
-algebras
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P})
on the choice of input data.

Let
{\Upgamma}
be a
\mathbb{C}
-algebra and let
M
be a periodic
\Upgamma
-module, as in
2.1
. We retain the notation there, in particular the complex
\mathrm{P}
of finitely generated projectives which induces an exact sequence

0\to M\xrightarrow{\upbeta}P_{n-1}\to P_{n-2}\to\dots\to P_{1}\to P_{0}\xrightarrow{\upalpha}M\to 0.

(4.A)

Remark 4.1
.

Let
\mathrm{P},\mathrm{P}^{\prime}
be two periodic projective resolutions of
M
, as in (
4.A
), of the same length.

(1)

There exists a chain map
g\colon\mathrm{P}\to\mathrm{P}^{\prime}
lifting the identity on
M
, which is unique up to chain homotopy in the following sense: given any other chain map
h\colon\mathrm{P}\to\mathrm{P}^{\prime}
lifting the identity on
M
there exists a chain homotopy
k
such that

g_{i}-h_{i}=\begin{cases}d_{1}^{\prime}\circ k_{0}&\mbox{if }i=0\\
d_{i+1}^{\prime}\circ k_{i}+k_{i-1}\circ d_{i}&\mbox{if }1\leq i\leq n-2\\
\upbeta^{\prime}\circ k_{n-1}+k_{n-2}\circ d_{n-1}&\mbox{if }i=n-1\\
\end{cases}

as illustrated in the following diagram

P_{n-1}
P_{n-2}
\ldots
P_{1}
P_{0}
\scriptstyle d_{n-1}
\scriptstyle d_{1}
0
M
P^{\prime}_{n-1}
P^{\prime}_{n-2}
\ldots
P^{\prime}_{1}
P^{\prime}_{0}
\scriptstyle\upbeta^{\prime}
\scriptstyle d_{n-1}^{\prime}
\scriptstyle d_{1}^{\prime}
\scriptstyle g_{1}-h_{1}
\scriptstyle g_{0}-h_{0}
\scriptstyle k_{n-1}
\scriptstyle k_{n-2}
\scriptstyle k_{1}
\scriptstyle k_{0}

(2)

It is important to note that in (
1
), the chain map
g\colon\mathrm{P}\to\mathrm{P}^{\prime}
that lifts the identity on
M
is not necessarily a quasi-isomorphism. This is because the map

0
M
P_{n-1}
\scriptstyle\upbeta
\scriptstyle d_{n-1}
0
M
P^{\prime}_{n-1}
\scriptstyle\upbeta^{\prime}
\scriptstyle d_{n-1}^{\prime}
\scriptstyle g_{n-1}

induced by
g_{n-1}
is not necessarily an isomorphism. We will show in
6.4
that this problem can be overcome in the case when
M
is simple.

(3)

Regardless of the above two points, which both involve lifting the identity of
M
, if there happens to exist a quasi-isomorphism
g\colon\mathrm{P}\to\mathrm{P}^{\prime}
, then
\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P})
and
\mathop{\mathcal{E}\rm nd}\nolimits_{\Upgamma}(\mathrm{P}^{\prime})
are quasi-isomorphic
DG
-algebras. This follows by e.g.
[
ST
, 4.4]
, since
g
is a homotopy equivalence by the projectivity of all the modules involved.

A periodic projective resolution of
M
may alternatively be thought of as an
n
-fold extension of
M
with itself formed by projective modules. In particular, according to the theory of
n
-fold extensions of Yoneda
[
Y
]
, it gives a class
\upvartheta\in\textnormal{Ext}^{n}_{\Upgamma}(M,M)
.

Definition 4.2
.

Let
\mathrm{P},\mathrm{P}^{\prime}
be two periodic projective resolutions of
M
, as in (
4.A
). A periodic quasi-isomorphism
g\colon\mathrm{P}\to\mathrm{P}^{\prime}
is a collection of maps which combine to give the following commutative diagram.

0
M
P_{n-1}
P_{n-2}
\ldots
P_{1}
P_{0}
M
0
\scriptstyle\upbeta
\scriptstyle\upalpha
0
M
P^{\prime}_{n-1}
P^{\prime}_{n-2}
\ldots
P^{\prime}_{1}
P^{\prime}_{0}
M
0
\scriptstyle\upbeta^{\prime}
\scriptstyle\upalpha^{\prime}
\scriptstyle g_{n-1}
\scriptstyle g_{n-2}
\scriptstyle g_{1}
\scriptstyle g_{0}

Note that a periodic quasi-isomorphism is in fact a quasi-isomorphism, given that it induces an isomorphism on the only two non-zero cohomologies.

Remark 4.3
.

Let
\upvartheta,\upvartheta^{\prime}
denote the Ext classes associated to the periodic projective resolutions
\mathrm{P},\mathrm{P}^{\prime}
respectively.
According to the theory of
n
-fold extensions of Yoneda, a periodic quasi-isomorphism
g\colon\mathrm{P}\to\mathrm{P}^{\prime}
gives an equivalence between the two extensions, and hence we have that
\upvartheta=\upvartheta^{\prime}
in
\textnormal{Ext}^{n}_{\Upgamma}(M,M)
.

The converse is also true.

Proposition 4.4
.

Two periodic projective resolutions of
M
